Column 1 (`tongue_y`) is the value; column 2 (`likelihood`) decides whether the tongue is visible in that frame.\n\nii.\n```python\nts = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series['Camera0_side_TongueTracking']\nt_cam = np.asarray(ts.timestamps)\ndata = ts.data[:]                        # (n_frames, 3) = x, y, likelihood\ny = data[:, 1].copy()\n```\n\niii. This is the only tongue measurement in the file. The channel layout is stated in the series' own `description` attribute (`\"('tongue_x', 'tongue_y', 'tongue_likelihood')\"`) rather than assumed. Tracking is present in all 174 sessions and runs at ~294 Hz.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *tongue_y_position*?\n\ni. Four steps. Frames with `likelihood < 0.5` are set to NaN, since the tracker still reports a position when the tongue is not protruding. The surviving values are averaged into 50 ms bins across the whole session, and the 40th and 60th percentiles of *those bin means* give two class edges. Each trial's window is then binned the same way and digitised against those edges, giving classes 0/1/2. Bins containing no visible frame are assigned a fourth class, `3` (`'not visible'`).\n\nii.\n```python\nTONGUE_CONF = 0.5                # tracking likelihood for the tongue to count as visible\nTONGUE_PCT = (40, 60)            # per-session percentiles -> 3 visible classes\nTONGUE_HIDDEN = 3                # no visible tongue frame in the bin\n```\n\n```python\ny[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n\n# bin the whole session on one grid, then take percentiles of the bin means\ngidx = np.floor((t_cam - t_cam[0]) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\nedges = np.nanpercentile(_bin_mean(gidx, y, int(gidx[-1]) + 1), TONGUE_PCT)\n\n# per trial, bin the same way and digitise against those edges\ncls = np.full((len(go), N_BINS), TONGUE_HIDDEN, np.int8)\nlo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\nhi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\nfor i in range(len(go)):\n    b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n    np.clip(b, 0, N_BINS - 1, out=b)\n    m = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\n    ok = ~np.isnan(m)\n    cls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n```\n\niii. The tongue is visible in only ~10% of frames, and when it is retracted the tracker still emits a position, so those frames are discarded rather than averaged in — including them shifts the percentiles and mixes real protrusions with noise. The likelihood value is effectively binary (89% of frames below 0.01, 10.5% at or above 0.99), so the exact threshold does not matter.\n\nPercentiles are taken over the 50 ms bin means rather than raw frames so the edges are defined on the same quantity that gets discretised; taking them over frames instead skews the resulting class balance, because averaging within a bin pulls values toward the center. The 40th/60th split and the per-session scope both follow the instructions. A fourth class is defined for bins with no visible tongue, which is 75% of all bins.\n\n## 8-d. How is `output` *tongue_y_position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. This is the one genuinely time-varying output, so it is the only one needing real alignment. The camera timestamps are on the same session-absolute clock as the spikes and the go cues, so each trial's frame range is found by `searchsorted` on the camera timestamps at `go + T_START` and `go + T_STOP`, and frames are assigned to bins by their offset from `go + T_START` — the same go-cue-relative grid used for the firing rates.\n\nii.\n```python\nlo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\nhi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\nfor i in range(len(go)):\n    b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The camera timestamps share the global clock with the spikes and events, so no interpolation or offset correction is needed — the same bin grid is applied to both streams, which guarantees bin `k` of the tongue output covers the same interval as bin `k` of the firing rates.\n\nUnlike the spikes, the video is trial-gated: it runs from `trials.start_time` to the trial-end event and is off during the inter-trial interval. On the ~3% of trials where the go cue falls less than 2.5 s after trial start, the leading bins therefore contain no frames at all and fall into the `'not visible'` class.\n\n## 9. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Three cases:\n\n- **Session never quality-controlled**: `classification` and `anno_name` are NaN rather than strings. `_text` maps any non-string entry to `''`, the session then has no `'good'` units, and it is dropped.\n- **Trials without spike data**: excluded via `obs_intervals` and `free_water` (see 1-e).\n- **Frames with no tracked tongue**: set to NaN and excluded from the bin mean; a bin left with no valid frame becomes the `'not visible'` class.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef _text(col):\n    \"\"\"Read a text column; non-str entries (unlabelled sessions) become ''.\"\"\"\n    return np.array([x if isinstance(x, str) else '' for x in col[:]])\n```\n\n```python\ngood = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\nif good.size == 0:\n    return None\n```\n\n```python\ny[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n...\nm = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\nok = ~np.isnan(m)\ncls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n```\n\niii. Missing data is handled one of two ways depending on what it means. Where nothing was recorded, the session or trial is excluded, since emitting it would fabricate data — a trial with no spikes would otherwise appear as 4 s of 0 Hz across every unit. Where the measurement legitimately has no value, as with a retracted tongue, it is represented as an explicit category rather than imputed.\n\n## 10-a. names of each output, of length n_output\\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'metadata': {\\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        ...\\n    }\\n}\\n```\\n\\n### Key Format Requirements\\n\\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \\n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\\n\\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \\n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \\n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\\n\\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\\n\\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \\n\\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\\n\\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \\n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \\n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\\n\\n## Python environment\\n\\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\\n\\n## Decoder Reference\\n\\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\\n- Optional arguments:\\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\\n\\n### Purpose\\n\\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\\n\\n### Consistency requirements\\n\\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\\n- Loading of data\\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\\n\\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \\n\\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \\n\\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder.\\n\\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \\n\\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference papers and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \\n\\n---\\n\\n## Conversion Workflow\\n\\n**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Each step has explicit completion criteria. Do not proceed to the next step until the current step is complete. Use the exact step names below when documenting progress in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n**STATUS TRACKING**: At the start of each step, update its status to `IN PROGRESS` in CONVERSION_NOTES.md. When complete, update to `COMPLETE`. This helps you and the user track progress.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 0: Setup and Initialization\\n\\n**Goal**: Set up the working environment and create the notes file.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. **FIRST**: Create `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` with the template structure shown at the end of this document\\n2. Verify you can run `python3` and import key packages (numpy, torch)\\n3. List the contents of this directory to understand what files are available\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists with the proper template structure\\n- You have confirmed Python and key packages work\\n- You have listed directory contents in CONVERSION_NOTES.md\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 1, verify that `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists by running `ls -la /app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. If it doesn't exist, STOP and create it now.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 1: Reference Code Exploration\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at code in the `/app/code` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\\n\\n**Goal**: Understand how the original authors loaded and processed their data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Look for documentation or README files in the `/app/code` directory\\n- Read the provided reference code bases to see how the reference papers loaded and manipulated the data\\n- Understand which functions are being called to read, curate, process, and manipulate the data\\n- For neural imaging data, does delta F over F need to be computed? \\n- For electrophysiology neural data, do cells need to be filtered based on quality? \\n- Note to file important functions, their inputs, and outputs\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented key functions and their purposes in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 1\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 2, update Step 1 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 2: Dataset Exploration\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at data in the `/app/data` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\\n\\n**Goal**: Understand the native data structure\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Identify how data is organized (all files, formats, hierarchies)\\n- Determine available variables and their meanings\\n- Check data dimensions and data types\\n- Look for documentation or README files\\n- Note the data organization and structure\\n- Note dataset size parameters such as the **total number** of subjects (mice), sessions, trials, and neurons\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented the data structure, file formats, size, and available variables in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 2\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 3, update Step 2 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 3: Reference Text Reading\\n\\n**Goal**: Extract key information from the papers and methods.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Scan the reference papers and methods.txt for important details about the data\\n- Look for and note to file:\\n  - Any information about dataset size (number of neurons, subjects, sessions, trials)\\n  - Fraction of data with different values, e.g. fraction of rewarded outcomes\\n  - Data processing, curation, and filtering\\n  - Variables important to experiments\\n  - Temporal alignment\\n  - Temporal binning\\n- Read the reference papers to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 3\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 4: Check for Consistency\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Compare your understanding of:\\n  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\\n  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\\n  - Analysis described in the reference texts\\n- Note and investigate any discrepancies\\n- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\\n  - Experimental and behavioral variables\\n  - Data processing\\n  - Data curation\\n- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 4\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 5: Mapping Planning\\n\\n**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Map source data elements to target format components:\\n  - Which variables contain neural activity?\\n  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\\n  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\\n  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `/app/code` directory. \\n- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\\n  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\\n  - Temporal alignment of trials\\n  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\\n  - Brain regions recorded from\\n- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\\n- Check for variables indicating valid data periods \\u2014 exclude invalid data\\n- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\\n- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented all decisions under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 6: Script Development\\n\\n**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `/app/convert_data.py`.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Write all conversion code in the file `/app/convert_data.py`\\n- Script should run as **`python -u /app/convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\\n- Include options:\\n  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\\n  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\\n  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\\n- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\\n- Use `pynwb` to interface with the data. Do not directly load and parse the `hdf5` files with `h5py`.\\n- Write efficient code:\\n  - Vectorize loops\\n  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\\n  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\\n  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\\n- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\\n  - There are no temporal misalignments\\n  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\\n- Write clear, well-documented functions\\n- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\\n- Validate data shapes and types at each step\\n- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\\n\\n**Done when**: `/app/convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `/app/convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_sample_out.txt`\\n  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\\n  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\\n  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \\n  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\\n2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\\n  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \\n  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \\n  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \\n3. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_sample_out.txt`.\\n4. Analyze the output file `/app/verification_sample_out.txt`:\\n  a. Verify no errors reported.\\n  b. Attempt to address any warnings\\n  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `/app/sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- Processing plots show no anomalies\\n- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 7\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `/app/sample_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\\n2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\\n\\n**Investigate any issues**:\\n- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- Decoder training completes without errors\\n- Loss decreases over epochs\\n- Accuracies are above chance\\n- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 8\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `/app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\\n2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\\n3. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_full_out.txt`\\n4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \\n5. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_full_out.txt`. \\n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \\n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `/app/verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- `/app/converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `/app/verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 9\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `/app/converted_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\\n\\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\\nRead through `/app/verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \\n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \\n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \\n- Document your sanity checks to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \\n- For each major processing step \\u2014 (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction \\u2014 identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \\n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \\n- Fix any mismatches. \\n- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\\n\\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \\n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \\n- Fix any discovered issues. \\n- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \\n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 10\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \\n2. **Wait for complete execution** \\u2014 this may take significant time for large datasets\\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \\n\\n**Done when**: \\n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 11\\\" with a table of accuracies\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\\n\\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \\n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \\n\\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers' accuracy for each variable\\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \\n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \\n\\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \\n\\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\\n2. Check temporal alignment \\u2014 plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \\n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\n**Report all checks and results** to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 12\\\".\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\\n\\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\\n  - Key decisions and rationale\\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\\n  - Validation results and tables\\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\\n- Create a user-facing `/app/README.md` summarizing:\\n  - Dataset description\\n  - How to load and use the converted data\\n  - Output format specification\\n  - Key statistics\\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `/app/cache/` folder\\n- Create `/app/cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\\n\\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\\n\\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Use `pynwb` to load data files.\\n2. Match the target data structure exactly\\n3. Preserve all relevant information from the source\\n4. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\\n5. Match information provided in the reference texts\\n6. Include complete and accurate metadata\\n7. Be reproducible with documented code\\n8. Include comprehensive CONVERSION_NOTES.md documenting all decisions and validation results\\n11. Outputs must match expert-written conversion code in:\\n  - Dataset size and distribution statistics\\n  - Decoder accuracy\\n12. Each decision about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. Decisions do not need to match the expert's: a different decision that is equally well justified is acceptable, while poorly justified decisions will be flagged as concerning or incorrect. cat /app/code/README.md\\r\\n# Video Analysis on the MAP dataset\\r\\n\\r\\nThis repository contains the analysis code that belongs to the paper:\\r\\n\\r\\n[Ziyue Aiden Wang*, Balint Kurgyis*, Susu Chen, Byungwoo Kang, Feng Chen, Yi Liu, Dave Liu, Karel Svoboda, Nuo Li, Shaul Druckmann: Brain-wide analysis reveals\\r\\nmovement encoding structured across and within brain areas ](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-025-02114-x).\\r\\n\\r\\nImplementation of the deep learning methods to train on a GPU cluster can be found in the repository [BehaviorVideo](https://github.com/kurgyis/BehaviorVideo).\\r\\n\\r\\nStandalone, lightweight implementation of the deep learning methods can be found in the repository: [VideoAnalysisTools](https://github.com/druckmann-lab/VideoA\\r\\nnalysisTools).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Data\\r\\n\\r\\nThe data used for this study has been publised and is available at:\\r\\nChen, S., et al. Mesoscale Activity Map Dataset. DANDI archive, 2023.  DOI: https://doi.org/10.48324/dandi.000363/0.231012.2129\\r\\n\\r\\nThe processed data that can be used to create the figures in the paper can be found in: [Processed data for MapVideoAnalysis](https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.167\\r\\n40783)\\r\\n\\r\\n## Installaion\\r\\n\\r\\nThe code found here can be run without installaion, just make sure that you import `local_env` in your notebooks or scripts that will add the VideoAnalysisUtils\\r\\n package to your path, such as:\\r\\n```\\r\\nimport local_env\\r\\nfrom VideoAnalysisUtils import ...\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nor can be installed with pip as:\\r\\n```\\r\\npip install -e .\\r\\n```\\r\\nby default this will also install the cpu environmental dependencies.\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are two python environments that were used for the code in this repository:\\r\\n- `cpu_venv`: a Python 3.8 environment that was used for most of the CPU scripts, and everything that is in the folder `Sherlock`.\\r\\n- `local_gpu_venv`: a Python 3.12 environment with Pytorch that was used for all notebooks in the folder `Notebooks`.\\r\\n\\r\\nTo set up and actvate these environments you can do (on Linux):\\r\\n```\\r\\n# CPU venv\\r\\npython3 -m venv .cpu_venv\\r\\nsource .cpu_venv/bin/activate\\r\\ndeactivate\\r\\n\\r\\n# (or) GPU venv\\r\\npython3 -m venv .local_gpu_venv\\r\\nsource .local_gpu_venv/bin/activate\\r\\ndeactivate\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nYou can install the dependencies:\\r\\n```\\r\\npip install --upgrade pip\\r\\n\\r\\n# install CPU-only requirements\\r\\nsource .cpu_venv/bin/activate\\r\\npip install -r requirements_cpu_venv.txt\\r\\ndeactivate\\r\\n\\r\\n# \\u2026and install GPU requirements\\r\\nsource .local_gpu_venv/bin/activate\\r\\npip install -r requirements_local_gpu_venv.txt\\r\\ndeactivate\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nYou will have to install the correct GPU-enabled Pytorch version by hand.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Navigating this repository\\r\\n\\r\\nThe sem\\r\\nimport VideoAnalysisUtils.preprocessing_utils as preprocessing_utils\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\r\\nImportant notes on reading MATLAB files using Python:\\r\\nWhen we read an Nx1 MATLAB cell into a length-N Python list/array/anything iterable (and potentially do something before storing it into an iterable, e.g. if th\\r\\ne cell contains indices, we might need to subtract 1 from each element since MATLAB counts from 1 and Python counts from 0), pay attention to the following thre\\r\\ne cases:\\r\\n1. Empty 0x1 cell. Need to manually define what it becomes in Python.\\r\\n2. 1x1 cell. Will be directly read as non-iterables such as str, int, float, etc. Need to manually convert it to an iterable with length 1.\\r\\n3. Nx1 cell (N >= 2). Usually it's fine to read and directly store it in Python.\\r\\n\\r\\nComment: in case 2, when using isinstance(data, data_type), if data could be a number, consider the possibility of data being either float or int, i.e. use\\r\\n    if isinstance(data, float) or isinstance(data, int):\\r\\n        do something\\r\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nplt.rcParams['pdf.fonttype'] = 42\\r\\nplt.rcParams['ps.fonttype'] = 42\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\ndef sliding_histogram(spikeTimes, begin_time, end_time, bin_width, stride, rate=True):\\r\\n    '''\\r\\n    Calculates the number of spikes for each unit in each sliding bin of width bin_width, strided by stride.\\r\\n    begin_time and end_time are treated as the lower- and upper- bounds for bin centers.\\r\\n    if rate=True, calculate firing rates instead of bin spikes.\\r\\n    '''\\r\\n    bin_begin_time = begin_time\\r\\n    bin_end_time = end_time\\r\\n    # This is to deal with cases where for e.g. (bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride is actually 43 but evaluates to 42.999999... due to numerical errors in flo\\r\\nating-point arithmetic.\\r\\n    if np.allclose((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride, math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1):\\r\\n        n_bins =  math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+2\\r\\n    else:\\r\\n        n_bins = math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1\\r\\n    binCenters = bin_begin_time + np.arange(n_bins)*stride\\r\\n\\r\\n    binIntervals = np.vstack((binCenters-bin_width/2, binCenters+bin_width/2)).T\\r\\n\\r\\n    # Count number of spikes for each sliding bin\\r\\n    binSpikes = np.asarray([[[np.sum(np.all([trial>=binInt[0], trial<binInt[1]], axis=0)) for binInt in binIntervals] for trial in unit]\\r\\n        for unit in spikeTimes]).swapaxes(0,-1)\\r\\n\\r\\n    if rate:\\r\\n        return binCenters, binSpikes/float(bin_width) # get firing rates\\r\\n    return binCenters, binSpikes\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\ndef process_one_sess(data_folder, file_path_list, bw, stride, begin_time=-3.0, end_time=3.5, save_folder=os.path.join(os.pardir, 'data_processed'), qc_mode='cla\\r\\nssifier'):\\r\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\r\\n    Process all probe files in one session.\\r\\n\\r\\n    Args:\\r\\n        data_folder (str): the folder containing all data including QC neuron ID files.\\r\\n        file_path_list: list of full file paths of all probes in this session.\\r\\n        bw (float): in sec.\\r\\n        stride (float): in sec.\\r\\n        qc_mode (str): 'classifier' or 'old'.\\r\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\r\\n    print(qc_mode)\\r\\n    if qc_mode == 'classifier':\\r\\n        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'goodunits')\\r\\n        print(qc_folder)\\r\\n    elif qc_mode == 'old':\\r\\n        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'DJ_GoodUnitsIdx_14regions_old_qc')\\r\\n    else:\\r\\n        raise ValueError('Wrong qc_mode! Values are all list with length n_neurons.\\r\\nroot@d4c5d08591f8:/app# clear; cat /app/methods.txt\\r\\nBehavior and video tracking\\r\\n\\r\\nMice performed an auditory delayed response task 21 (Figure 1A). The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencie\\r\\ns (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An audi\\r\\ntory \\u2018Go\\u2019 cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\\r\\nquency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response e\\r\\npoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port trigge\\r\\nred a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, fol\\r\\nlowed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\\r\\nresponse trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any\\r\\n early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct\\r\\nlick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods\\r\\n). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\\r\\n\\r\\nTwo CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped wi\\r\\nth 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acqu\\r\\nired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\\r\\n\\r\\nMulti-regional electrophysiological recordings\\r\\n\\r\\nWe designed a \\ufb02exible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropi\\r\\nxels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1  1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the \\ufb01rst recording session. Deta\\r\\nils of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Targ\\r\\net regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, media\\r\\nl-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\\r\\n\\r\\nExtracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings\\r\\n were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain\\r\\ntissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\\r\\n\\r\\nProbes were con\\ufb01gured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). Dat\\r\\na from up to \\ufb01ve simultaneously recorded probes were acquired at 30 kHz. Sync waves (0.5 s duty cycle TTL pulses) were recorded across probes and on auxiliary c\\r\\nhannels for synchronization.\\r\\n\\r\\nPhotoinhibition\\r\\n\\r\\nLight from two 473 nm lasers (Coherent OBIS LS/LX) was used to illuminate the two hemispheres of ALM. The laser power was controlled by analog outputs controlle\\r\\nd by a Bpod (Sanworks). Photoinactivation of ALM (centered on AP 2.5 mm; ML 1.5 mm) was performed through clear-skull cap implant11 or via craniotomy by directi\\r\\nng the laser over the skull (beam diameter, 1.8 mm at 4 sigma). Photoinhibition of the left-, right-ALM hemisphere, or both hemispheres was deployed on a subset\\r\\n of ~25% randomly interleaved trials 11,21 (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials usin\\r\\ng visual cues, a \\u2018masking \\ufb02ash\\u2019 (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. For silencing ALM, we s\\r\\ntimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal pro\\ufb01le (5 mW average power per hemisphere\\r\\n) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons\\r\\n in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before\\r\\nthe \\u2018Go\\u2019 cue.\\r\\n\\r\\nTo analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostim\\r\\nulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). To exclude the effects of rebound population activity in\\r\\n the thalamus 16 and other subcortical regions, we used a 20-120 ms\\r\\ntime window after photo stimulation onset to measure the effect of ALM inactivation in downstream regions (Figures 5 and S5). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.\\r\\ne. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\\r\\n\\r\\nSpike sorting and quality control\\r\\n\\r\\nWe developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular\\r\\n recording traces were \\ufb01rst band-pass \\ufb01ltered (Butterworth acausal \\ufb01lter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). We then sorted the dataset using both Kilosort2 32 (MATLAB version, G\\r\\nitHub - jenniferColonell/KS20_for_preprocessed_data: Release version of KS2 for preprocessed data) and Kilosort2.5 4 (Python version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/\\r\\npykilosort: [WIP] Python port of Kilosort 2). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosor\\r\\nt2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://gi\\r\\nthub.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\\r\\n\\r\\nThe details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). In brief, we u\\r\\nsed 15 cluster quality metrics to train classi\\ufb01ers - one for each of the major \\ufb01ve brain areas (cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain and medulla). The classi\\ufb01er\\r\\ns were trained based on labels derived from manual curation. We curated Kilosort2 output (28 penetrations from \\ufb01ve brain areas) using the open-source graphical\\r\\nuser interface Phy (GitHub - cortex-lab/phy: phy: interactive visualization and manual spike sorting of large-scale ephys data). Each cluster was labeled as a \\u2018\\r\\ngood\\u2019 or \\u2018unlabeled\\u2019 unit. Annotators were blind to location, recording condition and trial type information. Based on the annotated labels of all clusters and\\r\\nassociated quality metrics, we trained \\ufb01ve region-speci\\ufb01c classi\\ufb01ers using logistic regression. Applying the trained classi\\ufb01ers to the rest of the dataset provi\\r\\nded lists of units that were labeled as \\u2018good\\u2019 and were used for the analysis in this paper. We applied the cortex classi\\ufb01er to ALM and other cortex, hippocampu\\r\\ns, olfactory and cortical subplate units; the striatum classi\\ufb01er to striatum and pallidum units; the thalamus classi\\ufb01er to thalamus and hypothalamus units; the\\r\\nmidbrain classi\\ufb01er to midbrain and pons units; the medulla classi\\ufb01er to medulla and cerebellum units. We calculated the rate at which the classi\\ufb01er incorrectly\\r\\nlabeled \\u2019unlabeled\\u2019 units as \\u2019good\\u2019, using 10-fold cross-validation on held-out manually labeled units (false alarm rate): 7.8%; striatum: 6.4%; thalamus: 7.3%;\\r\\n midbrain: 5.5%; medulla 4.3%. This method yielded 8717 good units from ALM, 7664 from striatum, 12808 from thalamus, 7495 from midbrain and 2928 from medulla,\\r\\netc. Overall, the dataset consisted of 69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions, from which 655 probe insertions were made. Signifi-\\r\\ncance was established through Benjamini\\u2013Hochberg false discovery\\r\\nrate control, with rate parameter Q\\u2009=\\u20090.05.\\r\\nDefining choice- and uninstructed movement-modulated\\r\\nneurons using video-based choice decoder\\r\\nT o define choice- and uninstructed movement-modulated neurons\\r\\n(Fig. 7 and Extended Data Fig. 9), we used all correct trials and split them\\r\\ninto four groups based on licking direction and the delay epoch video\\r\\nprediction (obtained as described above). The prediction was characterized by ROC AUC on\\r\\nthe test-fold and the two conditions were averaged in each case, yielding\\r\\na single AUC for choice prediction and another AUC for uninstructed\\r\\nmovement prediction for each neuron. T o classify a neuron\\r\\nas choice-modulated we used the same threshold as for the other\\r\\nmethod, AUC\\u2009>\\u20090.65.\\r\\nCalinski\\u2013Harabasz clustering score\\r\\nThe Calinski\\u2013Harabasz index, also known as the variance ratio criterion,\\r\\nis the ratio of the sum of between-clusters dispersion of a measured\\r\\nfeature to the inter-cluster dispersion for all clusters. Higher scores\\r\\nindicate stronger clustering. T o obtain a reference null-distribution\\r\\nfor the Calinski\\u2013Harabasz score, we randomly assign the labels (mice)\\r\\nto each data point, and then calculate the score, repeating the random\\r\\nprocess 1,000 times. This is recorded in `metadata`.\\n6. **Tongue visibility threshold 0.9** on the DLC likelihood: the likelihood distribution is strongly bimodal (frac > 0.5 = 0.1059, > 0.9 = 0.1052, > 0.99 = 0.1045 in an example session), so the result is insensitive to the exact threshold.\\n7. **Per-bin aggregation of the tongue marker**: the reference took the *last* video frame in each (3.4 ms) bin. the reference preprocessing code\\nCOARSE_REGIONS = ['ALM', 'Medulla', 'Midbrain', 'Striatum', 'Thalamus', 'Pons', 'Cerebellum',\\n                  'Hypothalamus', 'Hippocampus', 'Orbital', {name: i for i, name in enumerate(BRAIN_REGIONS)}\\n\\n\\n# ------------------------------------------------------------------- CCF region mapping\\ndef coarse_region(anno):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Map a fine CCF annotation (units.anno_name) onto one of the 14 coarse groups that\\n    the reference code (preprocessing_DJ_2022Aug.process_one_sess) uses.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    a = str(anno).strip()\\n    al = a.lower()\\n    if a == '':\\n        return None\\n    if any(k in al for k in ['lobule', 'lobules', 'copula pyramidis', 'nodulus', 'uvula', 'declive',\\n                             'folium', 'pyramus', 'flocculus', 'paramedian lobule', 'crus 1', 'crus 2',\\n                             'ansiform', 'cerebell', 'fastigial', 'interposed nucleus', 'dentate nucleus',\\n                             'central lobule', 'culmen', 'vermal', 'hemispheric', 'lingula',\\n                             'infracerebellar']):\\n        return 'Cerebellum'\\n    if any(k in al for k in ['medulla', 'medullary reticular', 'gigantocellular', 'paragigantocellular',\\n                             'magnocellular reticular', 'intermediate reticular', 'parvicellular reticular',\\n                             'spinal nucleus of the trigeminal', 'spinal vestibular', 'medial vestibular',\\n                             'lateral vestibular', 'inferior olivary', 'nucleus of roller', 'nucleus x',\\n                             'nucleus y', 'nucleus prepositus', 'raphe obscurus', 'raphe pallidus',\\n                             'raphe magnus', 'hypoglossal', 'facial motor nucleus', 'nucleus ambiguus',\\n                             'cuneate nucleus', 'gracile nucleus', 'solitary', 'dorsal motor nucleus',\\n                             'linear nucleus of the medulla', 'parasolitary', 'perihypoglossal',\\n                             'intercalated nucleus', 'lateral reticular nucleus', 'nucleus of the trapezoid',\\n                             'paratrigeminal', 'parapyramidal', 'area postrema', 'dorsal cochlear',\\n                             'ventral cochlear', 'efferent cochlear', 'vestibulocochlear']):\\n        return 'Medulla'\\n    if any(k in al for k in ['pons', 'pontine', 'koelliker-fuse', 'parabrachial', 'superior vestibular',\\n                             'locus ceruleus', 'lateral lemniscus', 'superior olivary', 'tegmental reticular',\\n                             'barrington', 'laterodorsal tegmental', 'sublaterodorsal', 'supratrigeminal',\\n                             'principal sensory nucleus of the trigeminal', 'motor nucleus of trigeminal',\\n                             'dorsal tegmental nucleus', 'nucleus incertus', 'pontine central gray',\\n                             'nucleus raphe pontis', 'peritrigeminal']):\\n        return 'Pons'\\n    if any(k in al for k in ['midbrain', 'superior colliculus', 'inferior colliculus', 'substantia nigra',\\n                             'red nucleus', 'pretectal', 'periaqueductal gray', 'pedunculopontine',\\n                             'accessory optic tract', 'nucleus of the optic tract', 'cuneiform',\\n                             'ventral tegmental area', 'interpeduncular', 'oculomotor', 'trochlear',\\n                             'edinger-westphal', 'nucleus of darkschewitsch', 'interstitial nucleus of cajal',\\n                             'midbrain trigeminal', 'dorsal raphe', 'nucleus sagulum', 'parabigeminal',\\n                             'retrorubral', 'anterior tegmental nucleus', 'nucleus of the brachium',\\n                             'subcommissural', 'periventricular gray']):\\n        return 'Midbrain'\\n    if any(k in al for k in ['field ca1', 'field ca2', 'field ca3', 'dentate gyrus', 'subiculum',\\n                             'postsubiculum', 'presubiculum', 'parasubiculum', 'prosubiculum',\\n                             'hippocamp', \\\"ammon's horn\\\", 'induseum griseum', 'fasciola cinerea']):\\n        return 'Hippocampus'\\n    if ('thalam' in al) or any(k in al for k in ['habenula', 'geniculate', 'parafascicular', 'paracentral nucleus',\\n                             'anteroventral nucleus', 'anterodorsal nucleus', 'anteromedial nucleus',\\n                             'rhomboid nucleus', 'submedial nucleus', 'suprageniculate', 'subparafascicular',\\n                             'nucleus of reunions', 'nucleus reuniens', 'reuniens', 'peripeduncular',\\n                             'intergeniculate', 'ethmoid nucleus', 'xiphoid', 'parataenial',\\n                             'interanteromedial', 'intermediodorsal', 'nucleus of the field',\\n                             'posterior triangular', 'perireunensis']):\\n        return 'Thalamus'\\n    if any(k in al for k in ['hypothalam', 'zona incerta', 'fields of forel', 'mammillary', 'supramammillary',\\n                             'tuberal', 'preoptic', 'subthalamic', 'arcuate hypothalamic',\\n                             'periventricular hypothalamic', 'median eminence', 'posterior hypothalamic']):\\n        return 'Hypothalamus'\\n    if any(k in al for k in ['globus pallidus', 'pallidum', 'substantia innominata',\\n                             'bed nuclei of the stria terminalis', 'bed nucleus of the anterior commissure',\\n                             'magnocellular nucleus', 'medial septal nucleus', 'diagonal band',\\n                             'triangular nucleus of septum']):\\n        return 'Pallidum'\\n    if any(k in al for k in ['caudoputamen', 'striatum', 'nucleus accumbens', 'fundus of striatum',\\n                             'central amygdalar', 'medial amygdalar', 'intercalated amygdalar',\\n                             'lateral septal', 'septofimbrial', 'septohippocampal',\\n                             'anterior amygdalar area', 'olfactory tubercle']):\\n        return 'Striatum'\\n    if any(k in al for k in ['claustrum', 'endopiriform', 'cortical subplate', 'basolateral amygdalar',\\n                             'basomedial amygdalar', 'lateral amygdalar', 'posterior amygdalar']):\\n        return 'CorticalSubplate'\\n    if any(k in al for k in ['olfactory', 'piriform', 'taenia tecta', 'lateral olfactory tract',\\n                             'cortical amygdalar area', 'postpiriform']):\\n        return 'Olfactory'\\n    if al.startswith('orbital area'):\\n        return 'Orbital'\\n    if al.startswith('secondary motor area') or al.startswith('primary motor area'):\\n        # probes were targeted at ALM (anterior lateral motor cortex); the reference groups\\n        # recorded motor-cortex units as ALM\\n        return 'ALM'\\n    if any(k in al for k in ['area', 'cortex', 'layer', 'field', 'frontal pole', 'ectorhinal', 'perirhinal',\\n                             'entorhinal', 'gustatory', 'visceral', 'insular', 'retrosplenial', 'cingulate',\\n                             'prelimbic', 'infralimbic', 'dorsal peduncular']):\\n        return 'OtherCortex'\\n    return None\\n\\n\\n# ------------------------------------------------------------------------- session code\\ndef process_session(path, make_plots=False):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Convert one NWB session. Returns a dict with per-session converted data, or None.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    t0 = time.time()\\n    timing = {}\\n    with NWBHDF5IO(path, mode='r', load_namespaces=True) as io:\\n        nwb = io.read()\\n        session_id = nwb.identifier\\n        subject_id = str(nwb.subject.subject_id)\\n        mouse_name = str(nwb.subject.description)\\n\\n        # ---------------- trials ----------------\\n        trials = nwb.intervals['trials']\\n        df = trials.to_dataframe()\\n        n_trials_all = len(df)\\n        start_time = df['start_time'].values.astype(float)\\n        stop_time = df['stop_time'].values.astype(float)\\n        outcome_str = np.asarray(df['outcome'].values, dtype=object)\\n        early_str = np.asarray(df['early_lick'].values, dtype=object)\\n        instruction = np.asarray(df['trial_instruction'].values, dtype=object)\\n        auto_water = np.asarray(df['auto_water'].values).astype(int)\\n        free_water = np.asarray(df['free_water'].values).astype(int)\\n\\n        be = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralEvents'].time_series\\n        go_times = np.asarray(be['go_start_times'].timestamps[:], dtype=float)\\n        sample_times = np.asarray(be['sample_start_times'].timestamps[:], dtype=float)\\n        left_licks = np.asarray(be['left_lick_times'].timestamps[:], dtype=float)\\n        right_licks = np.asarray(be['right_lick_times'].timestamps[:], dtype=float)\\n        if 'photostim_start_times' in be:\\n            stim_on = np.asarray(be['photostim_start_times'].timestamps[:], dtype=float)\\n            stim_off = np.asarray(be['photostim_stop_times'].timestamps[:], dtype=float)\\n        else:\\n            stim_on = np.zeros(0)\\n            stim_off = np.zeros(0)\\n\\n        if len(go_times) != n_trials_all:\\n            # defensive: keep only trials that contain exactly one go cue\\n            raise RuntimeError('%s: %d go cues for %d trials' % (session_id, len(go_times), n_trials_all))\\n\\n        # ---- trial curation (reference get_regular_trial_mask, minus the exclusions that\\n        #      the decoder task requires us to keep: early lick, no-response, photostim) ----\\n        keep = (auto_water == 0) & (free_water == 0)\\n        # the go cue must lie inside the trial (sanity)\\n        keep &= (go_times >= start_time) & (go_times <= stop_time)\\n        trial_idx = np.where(keep)[0]\\n        n_trials = len(trial_idx)\\n        if n_trials < 2:\\n            return None\\n\\n        go = go_times[trial_idx]\\n        tstart = start_time[trial_idx]\\n        tstop = stop_time[trial_idx]\\n\\n        # absolute bin edges, clipped to the observed (trial) interval so that no activity\\n        # from neighbouring trials can leak in; unobserved parts of the window stay empty\\n        edges = go[:, None] + BIN_EDGES_REL[None, :]           # (n_trials, NBINS+1)\\n        edges_clipped = np.clip(edges, tstart[:, None], tstop[:, None])\\n        observed = (edges[:, 1:] <= tstop[:, None]) & (edges[:, :-1] >= tstart[:, None])\\n\\n        # ---------------- units ----------------\\n        units = nwb.units\\n        classification = np.asarray([str(c) for c in units['classification'][:]])\\n        anno = np.asarray([str(a) for a in units['anno_name'][:]])\\n        good = classification == 'good'\\n        if good.sum() == 0:\\n            return None\\n\\n        # unit -> electrode (exactly one electrode per unit) -> CCF ML coordinate\\n        vi = units['electrodes']\\n        offs = np.asarray(vi.data[:])\\n        starts = np.concatenate([[0], offs[:-1]])\\n        elec_row = np.asarray(vi.target.data[:])[starts]\\n        electrodes = nwb.electrodes.to_dataframe()\\n        ml = electrodes['x'].values.astype(float)[elec_row]\\n\\n        region_idx = np.full(len(classification), -1, dtype=np.int64)\\n        for i in np.where(good)[0]:\\n            reg = coarse_region(anno[i])\\n            if reg is None or not np.isfinite(ml[i]):\\n                continue\\n            side = 'left' if ml[i] >= ML_MIDLINE else 'right'   # reference helper_get_neuron_id_area\\n            region_idx[i] = BRAIN_REGION_INDEX['%s %s' % (side, reg)]\\n        use_unit = good & (region_idx >= 0)\\n        unit_ids = np.where(use_unit)[0]\\n        n_units = len(unit_ids)\\n        if n_units == 0:\\n            return None\\n        timing['setup'] = time.time() - t0\\n\\n        # ---------------- neural: spike counts -> firing rate (Hz) ----------------\\n        t1 = time.time()\\n        flat_edges = edges_clipped.ravel()\\n        rates = np.zeros((n_units, n_trials, NBINS), dtype=np.float32)\\n        spike_index = units['spike_times']\\n        for k, ui in enumerate(unit_ids):\\n            st = np.asarray(spike_index[int(ui)], dtype=float)\\n            pos = np.searchsorted(st, flat_edges).reshape(n_trials, NBINS + 1)\\n            rates[k] = np.diff(pos, axis=1).astype(np.float32)\\n        rates /= BIN_SIZE                                        # spikes/s, as in the reference\\n        timing['neural'] = time.time() - t1\\n\\n        # ---------------- inputs ----------------\\n        t1 = time.time()\\n        # (a) time from tone (sample epoch) onset. Early-lick trials replay the sample epoch,\\n        #     so the tone that preceded a given go cue is the LAST sample onset before it.\\n        pos = np.searchsorted(sample_times, go, side='left') - 1\\n        tone_onset = np.where(pos >= 0, sample_times[np.clip(pos, 0, len(sample_times) - 1)], np.nan)\\n        bad_tone = ~np.isfinite(tone_onset) | (tone_onset < tstart - 1e-9)\\n        n_bad_tone = int(bad_tone.sum())\\n        tone_onset = np.where(bad_tone, go - 1.85, tone_onset)   # fallback: standard structure\\n        time_from_tone = (go - tone_onset)[:, None] + BIN_CENTERS_REL[None, :]   # (n_trials, NBINS)\\n\\n        # (b) photostimulation on/off at each time point\\n        photostim = np.zeros((n_trials, NBINS), dtype=np.float32)\\n        if len(stim_on):\\n            which = np.searchsorted(tstart, stim_on, side='right') - 1\\n            for s_on, s_off, w in zip(stim_on, stim_off, which):\\n                if w < 0 or w >= n_trials:\\n                    continue\\n                if s_on < tstart[w] or s_on > tstop[w]:\\n                    continue\\n                ov = (edges[w, :-1] < s_off) & (edges[w, 1:] > s_on)\\n                photostim[w] |= ov\\n        inputs = np.stack([time_from_tone.astype(np.float32), photostim], axis=1)  # (n_trials, 2, NBINS)\\n        timing['input'] = time.time() - t1\\n\\n        # ---------------- outputs ----------------\\n        t1 = time.time()\\n        # (a) lick direction choice: direction of the first lick after the go cue\\n        choice = np.full(n_trials, 2, dtype=np.int64)            # 2 = no lick\\n        for i in range(n_trials):\\n            l = left_licks[(left_licks >= go[i]) & (left_licks <= tstop[i])]\\n            r = right_licks[(right_licks >= go[i]) & (right_licks <= tstop[i])]\\n            if len(l) == 0 and len(r) == 0:\\n                continue\\n            if len(r) == 0:\\n                choice[i] = 0\\n            elif len(l) == 0:\\n                choice[i] = 1\\n            else:\\n                choice[i] = 0 if l[0] < r[0] else 1\\n\\n        # (b) outcome\\n        omap = {'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}\\n        outcome = np.array([omap[str(o)] for o in outcome_str[trial_idx]], dtype=np.int64)\\n\\n        # (c) early lick\\n        early = np.array([1 if str(e) == 'early' else 0 for e in early_str[trial_idx]], dtype=np.int64)\\n\\n        # (d) tongue y position (DeepLabCut side camera, 300 Hz)\\n        ts_obj = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series['Camera0_side_TongueTracking']\\n        vts = np.asarray(ts_obj.timestamps[:], dtype=float)\\n        vdata = np.asarray(ts_obj.data[:], dtype=float)\\n        tongue_y = vdata[:, 1]\\n        visible = vdata[:, 2] > LIKELIHOOD_THRESH\\n        ysum = np.concatenate([[0.0], np.cumsum(np.where(visible, tongue_y, 0.0))])\\n        vcnt = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(visible.astype(np.int64))])\\n        vidx = np.searchsorted(vts, edges_clipped)               # (n_trials, NBINS+1)\\n        s_y = ysum[vidx[:, 1:]] - ysum[vidx[:, :-1]]\\n        n_y = vcnt[vidx[:, 1:]] - vcnt[vidx[:, :-1]]\\n        with np.errstate(invalid='ignore', divide='ignore'):\\n            ybin = np.where(n_y > 0, s_y / np.maximum(n_y, 1), np.nan)\\n        vis_vals = ybin[np.isfinite(ybin)]\\n        if vis_vals.size >= 10:\\n            p40, p60 = np.percentile(vis_vals, [40, 60])\\n        else:\\n            p40 = p60 = np.nan\\n        tongue_cls = np.full((n_trials, NBINS), 3, dtype=np.int64)    # 3 = not visible\\n        if np.isfinite(p40):\\n            fin = np.isfinite(ybin)\\n            tongue_cls[fin & (ybin < p40)] = 0\\n            tongue_cls[fin & (ybin >= p40) & (ybin <= p60)] = 1\\n            tongue_cls[fin & (ybin > p60)] = 2\\n\\n        outputs = np.empty((n_trials, 4, NBINS), dtype=np.int64)\\n        outputs[:, 0, :] = choice[:, None]\\n        outputs[:, 1, :] = outcome[:, None]\\n        outputs[:, 2, :] = early[:, None]\\n        outputs[:, 3, :] = tongue_cls\\n        timing['output'] = time.time() - t1\\n\\n        result = {\\n            'session_id': session_id,\\n            'subject_id': subject_id,\\n            'mouse_name': mouse_name,\\n            'path': path,\\n            'neural': [np.ascontiguousarray(rates[:, i, :]) for i in range(n_trials)],\\n            'input': [np.ascontiguousarray(inputs[i]) for i in range(n_trials)],\\n            'output': [np.ascontiguousarray(outputs[i]) for i in range(n_trials)],\\n            'brain_region_idx': region_idx[unit_ids].astype(np.int64),\\n            'n_units_total': int(len(classification)),\\n            'n_units_good': int(good.sum()),\\n            'n_units_used': int(n_units),\\n            'n_trials_all': int(n_trials_all),\\n            'n_trials_used': int(n_trials),\\n            'n_trials_autowater': int((auto_water > 0).sum()),\\n            'n_trials_freewater': int((free_water > 0).sum()),\\n            'frac_fully_observed': float(np.mean(observed.all(axis=1))),\\n            'frac_bins_observed': float(np.mean(observed)),\\n            'n_bad_tone': n_bad_tone,\\n            'tongue_pct': (float(p40), float(p60)),\\n            'timing': timing,\\n            'total_time': time.time() - t0,\\n        }\\n\\n        if make_plots:\\n            plot_session(result, dict(go=go, tstart=tstart, tstop=tstop, tone_onset=tone_onset,\\n                                      vts=vts, tongue_y=tongue_y, visible=visible,\\n                                      ybin=ybin, p40=p40, p60=p60, spike_index=spike_index,\\n                                      unit_ids=unit_ids, edges=edges, observed=observed,\\n                                      left_licks=left_licks, right_licks=right_licks,\\n                                      stim_on=stim_on, stim_off=stim_off, choice=choice,\\n                                      outcome=outcome, early=early))\\n    return result\\n\\n\\n# ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ plotting\\ndef plot_session(res, aux):\\n    import matplotlib\\n    matplotlib.use('Agg')\\n    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt\\n\\n    go = aux['go']\\n    n_trials = len(go)\\n    # pick a few illustrative trials: one hit, one miss, one ignore, one photostim if possible\\n    picks = []\\n    for cond, name in [((aux['outcome'] == 2), 'hit'), ((aux['outcome'] == 1), 'miss'),\\n                       ((aux['outcome'] == 0), 'ignore'),\\n                       ((np.array([res['input'][i][1].any() for i in range(n_trials)])), 'photostim')]:\\n        idx = np.where(cond)[0]\\n        if len(idx):\\n            picks.append((int(idx[len(idx) // 2]), name))\\n    if not picks:\\n        picks = [(0, 'trial0')]\\n\\n    fig, axes = plt.subplots(5, len(picks), figsize=(6 * len(picks), 16), squeeze=False)\\n    for col, (tr, name) in enumerate(picks):\\n        g = go[tr]\\n        # --- 1. spike raster (raw spike times relative to go cue) ---\\n        ax = axes[0][col]\\n        nshow = min(40, len(aux['unit_ids']))\\n        for j, ui in enumerate(aux['unit_ids'][:nshow]):\\n            st = np.asarray(aux['spike_index'][int(ui)], dtype=float) - g\\n            st = st[(st >= T_START) & (st < T_STOP)]\\n            ax.plot(st, np.full(len(st), j), '|', color='k', markersize=3)\\n        ax.axvline(0, color='r', label='go cue')\\n        ax.axvline(aux['tone_onset'][tr] - g, color='b', label='tone onset')\\n        ax.axvline(aux['tstop'][tr] - g, color='gray', ls='--', label='trial end')\\n        ax.set_xlim(T_START, T_STOP)\\n        ax.set_title('%s / trial %d (%s)\\\\nraw spikes (40 units)' % (res['session_id'], tr, name))\\n        ax.set_ylabel('unit')\\n        ax.legend(fontsize=6, loc='upper left')\\n\\n        # --- 2. binned firing rates ---\\n        ax = axes[1][col]\\n        im = ax.imshow(res['neural'][tr][:nshow], aspect='auto', origin='lower',\\n                       extent=[T_START, T_STOP, 0, nshow], cmap='magma')\\n        ax.axvline(0, color='c')\\n        ax.set_title('binned firing rate (Hz), 50 ms bins')\\n        ax.set_ylabel('unit')\\n        plt.colorbar(im, ax=ax, fraction=0.03)\\n\\n        # --- 3. inputs ---\\n        ax = axes[2][col]\\n        ax.plot(BIN_CENTERS_REL, res['input'][tr][0], '.-', label='time from tone onset (s)')\\n        ax.plot(BIN_CENTERS_REL, res['input'][tr][1], '.-', label='photostim')\\n        ax.axvline(0, color='r')\\n        ax.axvline(aux['tone_onset'][tr] - g, color='b')\\n        for s_on, s_off in zip(aux['stim_on'], aux['stim_off']):\\n            if aux['tstart'][tr] <= s_on <= aux['tstop'][tr]:\\n                ax.axvspan(s_on - g, s_off - g, color='violet', alpha=0.3)\\n        ax.set_title('inputs (vertical lines: go cue red, tone blue; shading: laser)')\\n        ax.legend(fontsize=7)\\n\\n        # --- 4. tongue tracking ---\\n        ax = axes[3][col]\\n        m = (aux['vts'] >= g + T_START) & (aux['vts'] < g + T_STOP)\\n        ax.plot(aux['vts'][m] - g, np.where(aux['visible'][m], aux['tongue_y'][m], np.nan),\\n                '.', ms=2, color='g', label='raw tongue y (visible frames)')\\n        ax.plot(BIN_CENTERS_REL, aux['ybin'][tr], 'o-', color='k', ms=3, label='binned mean y')\\n        ax.axhline(aux['p40'], color='orange', ls='--', label='40th pct')\\n        ax.axhline(aux['p60'], color='red', ls='--', label='60th pct')\\n        ax.axvline(0, color='r')\\n        ax.set_title('tongue y position and session percentiles')\\n        ax.legend(fontsize=6)\\n\\n        # --- 5. outputs ---\\n        ax = axes[4][col]\\n        for k in range(4):\\n            ax.step(BIN_CENTERS_REL, res['output'][tr][k] + 5 * k, where='mid',\\n                    label='%s (+%d)' % (OUTPUT_NAMES[k], 5 * k))\\n        for t in aux['left_licks'][(aux['left_licks'] > g + T_START) & (aux['left_licks'] < g + T_STOP)]:\\n            ax.axvline(t - g, color='b', alpha=0.25)\\n        for t in aux['right_licks'][(aux['right_licks'] > g + T_START) & (aux['right_licks'] < g + T_STOP)]:\\n            ax.axvline(t - g, color='m', alpha=0.25)\\n        ax.axvline(0, color='r')\\n        ax.set_title('outputs (blue=left licks, magenta=right licks)')\\n        ax.set_xlabel('time from go cue (s)')\\n        ax.legend(fontsize=6)\\n\\n    fig.tight_layout()\\n    fname = 'processing_%s.png' % res['session_id']\\n    fig.savefig(fname, dpi=110)\\n    plt.close(fig)\\n\\n    # --- session-level summary figure ---\\n    fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 4, figsize=(22, 4))\\n    psth = np.mean(np.stack([n.mean(axis=0) for n in res['neural']]), axis=0)\\n    axes[0].plot(BIN_CENTERS_REL, psth)\\n    axes[0].axvline(0, color='r')\\n    axes[0].set_title('population mean firing rate (Hz)')\\n    axes[0].set_xlabel('time from go cue (s)')\\n    outs = np.stack(res['output'])\\n    axes[1].plot(BIN_CENTERS_REL, (outs[:, 3, :] != 3).mean(axis=0))\\n    axes[1].axvline(0, color='r')\\n    axes[1].set_title('fraction of trials with tongue visible')\\n    axes[2].plot(BIN_CENTERS_REL, np.stack(res['input'])[:, 1, :].mean(axis=0))\\n    axes[2].axvline(0, color='r')\\n    axes[2].set_title('fraction of trials with photostim on')\\n    vals, cnts = np.unique(outs[:, 3, :], return_counts=True)\\n    axes[3].bar(vals, cnts / cnts.sum())\\n    axes[3].set_xticks(range(4))\\n    axes[3].set_xticklabels(OUTPUT_VALUES[3], rotation=30)\\n    axes[3].set_title('tongue class distribution')\\n    fig.tight_layout()\\n    fig.savefig('processing_%s_summary.png' % res['session_id'], dpi=110)\\n    plt.close(fig)\\n\\n\\ndef _worker(args):\\n    path, make_plots = args\\n    try:\\n        res = process_session(path, make_plots=make_plots)\\n    except Exception as exc:  # noqa\\n        import traceback\\n        traceback.print_exc()\\n an auditory\\ngo cue (6 kHz, 0.1 s) opened a 1.5 s answer period in which the mouse licked one of two ports.\\nOn ~20% of randomly interleaved trials, ALM was photoinhibited during the last 0.5 s of the\\ndelay. Neuropixels probes (2-5 simultaneously) recorded brain-wide activity; the orofacial\\nmovements were tracked at 300 Hz with DeepLabCut.\\n\\n## Converted files\\n\\n| File | Content |\\n|---|---|\\n| `converted_data.pkl` | full dataset (173 sessions, 11.9 GB) |\\n| `sample_data.pkl` | 2 sessions, for quick tests |\\n| `convert_data.py` | conversion script (`python -u convert_data.py out.pkl [--full|--sample] [--show-processing]`) |\\n| `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` | full record of all decisions, checks and validation results |\\n| `conversion_full_out.txt`, `verification_full_out.txt`, `train_decoder_full_out.txt` | logs |\\n| `cache/` | exploration, sanity-check and analysis scripts |\\n\\n## How to load\\n\\n```python\\nimport pickle\\nwith open('/app/converted_data.pkl', 'rb') as f:\\n    data = pickle.load(f)\\n\\nneural = data['neural'][session][trial]   # (n_neurons, 80) firing rate in Hz\\ninp    = data['input'][session][trial]    # (2, 80)\\nout    = data['output'][session][trial]   # (4, 80) integer class labels\\n```\\n\\n## Format specification\\n\\n- **Alignment**: go cue onset. `input[s][t]`: `(2, 80)` float32\\n  | idx | name | description |\\n  |---|---|---|\\n  | 0 | `time_from_tone_onset` | seconds since the onset of the tone (sample epoch) that preceded the go cue; 1.85 s at the go cue in standard trials |\\n  | 1 | `photostim` | 1 while the ALM photoinhibition laser is on, else 0 |\\n- `output[s][t]`: `(4, 80)` int64\\n  | idx | name | values |\\n  |---|---|---|\\n  | 0 | `choice` | 0 left, 1 right, 2 no lick (direction of the first lick in the answer period; constant in time) |\\n  | 1 | `outcome` | 0 ignore, 1 miss, 2 hit (constant in time) |\\n  | 2 |
